---
title: "Electron–Positron Annihilations"
source_pdf: "D:\\GitHub\\books\\dokumen.pub_gauge-theories-of-the-strong-weak-and-electromagnetic-interactions-second-edition-secondnbsped-9781400848225.pdf"

> [!TLDR]
> Electron–positron annihilation measures neutral-current couplings through cross sections, angular asymmetries, and fermion polarization.

<!-- PDF page 212; printed page 198 -->

Because of the simplicity and definiteness of the parton model in this situation, we begin our discussion of electroweak processes involving hadrons by considering electron-positron interactions. The contribution of direct-channel $\gamma$- and $Z^0$-exchange diagrams (figure 6.22) to the reaction

$$
e^+e^-\to\mu^+\mu^- \tag{7.3.1}
$$

has already been presented in (6.4.27). The differential cross section for the parton-model reaction

$$
e^+e^-\to q\bar q\to\text{hadrons} \tag{7.3.2}
$$

differs only by an overall factor of $N_c=3$ for quark color and in the replacement of the electroweak couplings of the muon by those of the quark in question. It is thus

$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d\mechanical{\sigma}}{dz}(e^+e^-\to q\bar q)=N_c\bigg\{&
\frac{\pi\alpha^2\electric{Q_q}^2}{2\energy{s}}(1+z^2)\\
&-\frac{\alpha\electric{Q_q}G_{\mathrm{F}}\mechanical{M_Z}^2(\energy{s}-\mechanical{M_Z}^2)}
{8\sqrt2[(\energy{s}-\mechanical{M_Z}^2)^2+\mechanical{M_Z}^2\energy{\Gamma}^2]}\\
&\quad\times[(R_e+L_e)(R_q+L_q)(1+z^2)+2(R_e-L_e)(R_q-L_q)z]\\
&+\frac{G_{\mathrm{F}}^2\mechanical{M_Z}^4\energy{s}}
{64\pi[(\energy{s}-\mechanical{M_Z}^2)^2+\mechanical{M_Z}^2\energy{\Gamma}^2]}\\
&\quad\times[(R_e^2+L_e^2)(R_q^2+L_q^2)(1+z^2)
+2(R_e^2-L_e^2)(R_q^2-L_q^2)z]\bigg\},
\end{aligned} \tag{7.3.3}
$$

where $z=\cos\theta_{\mathrm{c.m.}}$ measures the angle between incoming and outgoing fermions. We will be concerned mostly with the consequences that arise in the standard $\mathrm{SU}(2)_{\mathrm{L}}\otimes\mathrm{U}(1)_{\mathrm{Y}}$ theory, but it is worth emphasizing that the forms (6.4.27) and (7.3.3) are valid for any theory containing a single neutral weak boson with vector and axial-vector couplings. We may test the standard model by reverting to the notation of the model-independent analysis introduced in section 6.4.

The cross section expected for the production of various species in the standard model with $x_{\mathrm{W}}=\sin^2\theta_{\mathrm{W}}=0.2122$ is plotted in figure 7.3 in terms of the quantity

$$
R\equiv\frac{\mechanical{\sigma}(e^+e^-\to f\bar f)}
{\mechanical{\sigma}_{\mathrm{QED}}(e^+e^-\to\mu^+\mu^-)}. \tag{7.3.4}
$$

The $Z^0$ has been assumed to decay with negligible kinematic suppression into three families of leptons and into $u$, $d$, $s$, $c$, and $b$ quark–antiquark pairs, and no radiative corrections have been made to the cross section. The $Z^0$ peak is quite unmistakable.

**Fig. 7.3** Energy dependence of the ratio $R\equiv\sigma(e^+e^-\to f\bar f)/\sigma_{\mathrm{QED}}(e^+e^-\to\mu^+\mu^-)$ in the $\mathrm{SU}(2)_{\mathrm{L}}\otimes\mathrm{U}(1)_{\mathrm{Y}}$ electroweak theory with $x_{\mathrm{W}}=0.2122$ and three generations of quarks and leptons: (a) $\ell^+\ell^-$ final state; (b) $\nu\bar\nu$ final state; (c) $u\bar u$ (charge $=\tfrac23$ quarks) final state; $d\bar d$ (charge $=-\tfrac13$ quarks) final state.


At the peak, the cross section for each fermion species is

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mechanical{\sigma}_{\mathrm{peak}}(e^+e^-\to f\bar f)
&=\frac{G_{\mathrm{F}}^2\mechanical{M_Z}^4(L_e^2+R_e^2)(L_f^2+R_f^2)N_c}
{24\pi\energy{\Gamma_Z}^2}\\
&=\frac{G_{\mathrm{F}}^2\mechanical{M_Z}^4(v_e^2+a_e^2)(v_f^2+a_f^2)N_c}
{6\pi\energy{\Gamma_Z}^2},
\end{aligned} \tag{7.3.5}
$$

where $N_c$ is the number of colors of the produced fermion $f$ and, in the second line, we employ the notation

$$
\left.\begin{aligned}
a_i&=\tfrac12(L_i-R_i)\\
v_i&=\tfrac12(L_i+R_i)
\end{aligned}\right\} \tag{7.3.6}
$$

used for model-independent analyses.

<!-- PDF page 214; printed page 200 -->

**Table 7.1** Cross Sections for Electron–Positron Annihilation at the $Z^0$ Peak.

| Channel | $\mechanical{\sigma}_{\mathrm{peak}}$ ($\unit{nb}$) | $R_{\mathrm{peak}}$ |
| --- | --- | --- |
| $\nu\bar\nu$ | 4.06 | 389 |
| $\mu^+\mu^-$ | 2.08 | 199 |
| $u\bar u$ | 7.24 | 693 |
| $d\bar d$ | 9.22 | 883 |
| hadrons | 42.1 | 4034 |

Thus, we may write the ratio (7.3.4) at the neutral gauge-boson peak as

$$
R_{\mathrm{peak}}(e^+e^-\to f\bar f)
=\frac{G_{\mathrm{F}}^2\mechanical{M_Z}^6(L_e^2+R_e^2)(L_f^2+R_f^2)N_c}
{32\pi^2\alpha^2\energy{\Gamma_Z}^2}. \tag{7.3.7}
$$

In the standard electroweak theory, with $\energy{\Gamma_Z}$ given by (7.2.13), we therefore have

$$
R_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(\nu\bar\nu)}
=\frac{9(1-4x_{\mathrm{W}}+8x_{\mathrm{W}}^2)}
{\alpha^2(21-40x_{\mathrm{W}}+160x_{\mathrm{W}}^2/3)^2} \tag{7.3.8}
$$

and

$$
R_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(\mu^+\mu^-)}
=(1-4x_{\mathrm{W}}+8x_{\mathrm{W}}^2)R_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(\nu\bar\nu)}, \tag{7.3.9}
$$

$$
R_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(u\bar u)}
=3\left(1-\frac{8x_{\mathrm{W}}}{3}+\frac{32x_{\mathrm{W}}^2}{3}\right)
R_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(\nu\bar\nu)}, \tag{7.3.10}
$$

$$
R_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(d\bar d)}
=3\left(1-\frac{4x_{\mathrm{W}}}{3}+\frac{8x_{\mathrm{W}}^2}{9}\right)
R_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(\nu\bar\nu)}. \tag{7.3.11}
$$

The resulting peak cross sections and ratios are given in table 7.1 for $x_{\mathrm{W}}=0.2122$ and five quark flavors (neglecting masses). The visible cross section is quite enormous with respect to the QED reference cross section of $10.45\,\unit{pb}$:

$$
R_{\mathrm{peak}}(e^+e^-\to\text{charged leptons or hadrons})\approx4630. \tag{7.3.12}
$$

Although this is reduced somewhat in practice by radiative corrections [9] and uncertainty in beam energy, the copious yield contributed to the immense productivity of the “$Z^0$ factories,” LEP (at CERN) and the Stanford Linear Collider. The measured cross sections for the reaction $e^+e^-\to\text{hadrons}$ over a wide range of energies are compiled in figure 7.4. Should one or more $Z'$ gauge bosons be found, they would provide inviting targets for a future lepton collider (cf. problem 7.11).

The peak cross section’s sensitivity to the total width of the $Z^0$ boson makes possible a determination of the number of universally coupled light neutrino species that complements the invisible-width approach of problem 7.6. Figure 7.5 compares expectations and observations for the $Z^0$ line shape; the data decisively select three light neutrino species over two or four.

<!-- PDF page 215; printed page 201 -->

![[gauge-theories-fig-7-4-hadronic-cross-section-source.png|**Fig. 7.4** The hadronic cross section in electron–positron annihilations as measured at a number of colliders (Ref. [10]). The curve is the standard-model prediction that corresponds, at leading order, to (7.3.3). The cross sections have been corrected for the effects of photon radiation.]]

![[gauge-theories-fig-7-5-neutrino-species-line-shape-source.png|**Fig. 7.5** LEP measurements of the hadron production cross section around the $Z^0$ resonance (Ref. [10]). The points are averages of measurements by the ALEPH, DELPHI, L3, and OPAL experiments, with uncertainties magnified by a factor of ten. The curves represent the line-shape expectations for two, three, and four neutrino species with standard-model couplings and negligible masses.]]

<!-- PDF page 216; printed page 202 -->

The most stringent constraint on the number of light neutrino species, $N_\nu=2.9840\pm0.0082$, comes from standard-model fits to the universe of LEP and SLC data [10]. It leaves little room for decays of $Z^0$ into exotic weakly interacting particles. The conclusion that only three active light species exist does not rule out a fourth generation of quarks and leptons, provided that the neutral leptons are heavy enough that their contributions to the invisible width would be negligible—if not zero! A fourth generation is constrained, but not excluded, by what we know of charged-current and neutral-current interactions [11].

A fruitful observable is the forward-backward asymmetry

$$
A\equiv\frac{\int_0^1 dz\,d\mechanical{\sigma}/dz-\int_{-1}^0 dz\,d\mechanical{\sigma}/dz}
{\int_{-1}^1 dz\,d\mechanical{\sigma}/dz}, \tag{6.4.28}
$$

already discussed for the charged leptons in section 6.4. In the low-energy approximation, the asymmetry is approximately given by

$$
A(q\bar q)=\frac{3G_{\mathrm{F}}\energy{s}}{16\pi\alpha\electric{Q_q}\sqrt2}
(R_e-L_e)(R_q-L_q). \tag{7.3.13}
$$

In the standard model, $(R_i-L_i)=\tau_3^{(i)}$, so that

$$
R_e-L_e=R_d-L_d=-(R_u-L_u)=1. \tag{7.3.14}
$$

Therefore, comparing with (6.4.29), we find

$$
\left.\begin{aligned}
A(u\bar u)&=\tfrac32 A(\mu^+\mu^-)\\
A(d\bar d)&=3A(\mu^+\mu^-)
\end{aligned}\right\}, \tag{7.3.15}
$$

where $u$ and $d$ are to be understood as generic symbols for the quarks of charge $+\tfrac23$ and $-\tfrac13$, respectively.

Thus, in experiments at $\sqrt{\energy{s}}=40\,\unit{GeV}$, the leading-order expectation is

$$
\left.\begin{aligned}
A(u\bar u)&\approx-16\%\\
A(d\bar d)&\approx-32\%
\end{aligned}\right\}, \tag{7.3.16}
$$

in the standard model. Measurements of these asymmetries—for example by tagging the decays of short-lived particles containing $c$ or $b$ quarks—can enable independent determinations of the neutral-current couplings of the heavy quarks. In the notation (7.3.6) of vector and axial couplings, the forward-backward asymmetry at low energies may be written as

$$
A(q\bar q)=\frac{3G_{\mathrm{F}}\energy{s}a_ea_q}{4\pi\alpha\electric{Q_q}\sqrt2}. \tag{7.3.17}
$$

Problem 7.12 illuminates the role of $A(b\bar b)$ in determining the weak isospin of the $b$ quark.

Although the low-energy forward-backward asymmetry can be a sensitive probe of neutral-current couplings, it is not directly sensitive to the *position* of the

**Fig. 7.6** Energy dependence of the forward-backward asymmetry (6.4.28) in electron–positron annihilations in the $\mathrm{SU}(2)_{\mathrm{L}}\otimes\mathrm{U}(1)_{\mathrm{Y}}$ electroweak theory with $x_{\mathrm{W}}=0.2122$. The solid curve refers to the $\mu^+\mu^-$ final state, the dashed curve, to $u\bar u$, and the dot-dashed curve, to $d\bar d$. The $Z^0$ has been assumed to decay with negligible kinematic suppression into three families of leptons and into $u$, $d$, $s$, $c$, and $b$ quark–antiquark pairs, and no radiative corrections are included.


$Z^0$-boson pole, as the absence of $\mechanical{M_Z}$ from (6.4.29), (6.4.31), (7.3.13), and (7.3.17) makes plain. The evolution of the asymmetry with increasing energy is, however, influenced by the $Z^0$ propagator, because the linear rise with $\energy{s}$ of the magnitude of the asymmetry is damped. The behavior anticipated in the standard model is illustrated in figure 7.6.

On the $Z^0$ peak, the asymmetry may be expressed as

$$
\begin{aligned}
A_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(f\bar f)}
&=\frac{3(L_e^2-R_e^2)(L_f^2-R_f^2)}
{4(L_e^2+R_e^2)(L_f^2+R_f^2)}\\
&=\frac{3a_ev_ea_fv_f}{(v_e^2+a_e^2)(v_f^2+a_f^2)}.
\end{aligned} \tag{7.3.18}
$$

In the standard model, we compute

$$
A_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(\mu^+\mu^-)}
=\frac34\left(\frac{1-4x_{\mathrm{W}}}{1-4x_{\mathrm{W}}+8x_{\mathrm{W}}^2}\right)^2, \tag{7.3.19a}
$$

$$
A_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(u\bar u)}
=\frac34\left(\frac{1-4x_{\mathrm{W}}}{1-4x_{\mathrm{W}}+8x_{\mathrm{W}}^2}\right)
\left(\frac{1-8x_{\mathrm{W}}/3}{1-8x_{\mathrm{W}}/3+32x_{\mathrm{W}}^2/9}\right), \tag{7.3.19b}
$$

$$
A_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(d\bar d)}
=\frac34\left(\frac{1-4x_{\mathrm{W}}}{1-4x_{\mathrm{W}}+8x_{\mathrm{W}}^2}\right)
\left(\frac{1-4x_{\mathrm{W}}/3}{1-4x_{\mathrm{W}}/3+8x_{\mathrm{W}}^2/9}\right). \tag{7.3.19c}
$$

The $Z^0$-pole asymmetries vanish for $x_{\mathrm{W}}=0.25$, where the vector coupling of the electron to the weak neutral current is zero, and so are highly sensitive to the value

**Fig. 7.7** Energy dependence of the net polarization of charged fermions produced in electron–positron annihilations in the $\mathrm{SU}(2)_{\mathrm{L}}\otimes\mathrm{U}(1)_{\mathrm{Y}}$ electroweak theory with $x_{\mathrm{W}}=0.2122$. The solid curve refers to the $\mu^+\mu^-$ final state, the dashed curve, to $u\bar u$, and the dot-dashed curve, to $d\bar d$. The $Z^0$ has been assumed to decay with negligible kinematic suppression into three families of leptons and into $u$, $d$, $s$, $c$, and $b$ quark–antiquark pairs, and no radiative corrections are included.


of the weak mixing parameter. For our example value, $x_{\mathrm{W}}=0.2122$, we would anticipate peak asymmetries $(0.07,\,0.16,\,0.21)$ for lepton pairs, uplike quarks, and downlike quarks, respectively. These are only in rough agreement with the measurements at LEP and SLC (Ref. [10]),

$$
A_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(\ell^+\ell^-)}=0.0171\pm0.0010, \tag{7.3.20a}
$$

$$
A_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(c\bar c)}=0.0707\pm0.0035, \tag{7.3.20b}
$$

$$
A_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(b\bar b)}=0.0992\pm0.0016; \tag{7.3.20c}
$$

but, as problem 7.9 will show, only a modest adjustment in the weak-mixing parameter, to $x_{\mathrm{W}}\approx0.232$, is required to reproduce the measurements. The full electroweak theory, including quantum corrections, is in impressive agreement with the entire suite of precision measurements. We introduce loop corrections in section 7.7.

Parity violation in the neutral-current interaction is manifested as a net polarization of the produced fermions,

$$
P=\frac{\mechanical{\sigma}_{\mathrm{R}}-\mechanical{\sigma}_{\mathrm{L}}}{\mechanical{\sigma}}. \tag{7.3.21}
$$

In the standard model with $x_{\mathrm{W}}=0.2122$, the polarization of all species is expected to be small at low energies, becoming appreciable in the neighborhood of the $Z^0$ resonance. This is shown in figure 7.7. On the $Z^0$ peak, the net polarization

<!-- PDF page 219; printed page 205 -->

(integrated over $z=\cos\theta$) is given by

$$
\begin{aligned}
P_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(f\bar f)}
&=\frac{R_f^2-L_f^2}{R_f^2+L_f^2}\\
&=\frac{-2a_fv_f}{v_f^2+a_f^2}.
\end{aligned} \tag{7.3.22}
$$

This means that the electron parameters could, in principle, be isolated in the ratio

$$
\frac{A_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(f\bar f)}}{P_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(f\bar f)}}
=\frac{-3a_ev_e}{2(v_e^2+a_e^2)}, \tag{7.3.23}
$$

without intervening universality assumptions. The $Z^0$-pole polarizations predicted at leading order are significant for our example value of $x_{\mathrm{W}}=0.2122$:

$$
P_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(\mu^+\mu^-)}
=\frac{4x_{\mathrm{W}}-1}{1-4x_{\mathrm{W}}+8x_{\mathrm{W}}^2}\to-0.30, \tag{7.3.24a}
$$

$$
P_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(u\bar u)}
=\frac{8x_{\mathrm{W}}/3-1}{1-8x_{\mathrm{W}}/3+32x_{\mathrm{W}}^2/9}\to-0.73, \tag{7.3.24b}
$$

$$
P_{\mathrm{peak}}^{(d\bar d)}
=\frac{4x_{\mathrm{W}}/3-1}{1-4x_{\mathrm{W}}/3+8x_{\mathrm{W}}^2/9}\to-0.95. \tag{7.3.24c}
$$

The negative sign of the polarization follows from our defining the polarization of a right-handed fermion as positive in a world in which left-handed couplings dominate. The polarization of the $\tau$-lepton is accessible to experiment through the kinematic distributions of the decay products and the V − A form of the charged-current interaction that mediates the decays. Problem 7.10 explores the information contained in the angular distribution of $\tau$ polarization on the $Z^0$ pole, an observable studied at LEP. The availability of longitudinally polarized electron or positron beams greatly expands the range of possible measurements, as we may learn from the basic formulas (6.4.27) and (7.3.3).

The four LEP experiments (ALEPH [12], DELPHI [13], L3 [14], and OPAL [15]) together recorded 17 million examples of $Z^0$ decay. Using polarized beams, the SLD [16] experiment at SLAC studied 600 thousand $Z^0$. Supplemented by inputs from neutrino scattering, hadron collider experiments, and atomic physics experiments, these experiments determined standard-model parameters, including chiral couplings of the $Z^0$-boson, with very remarkable precision [10]. Operating above the $W^+W^-$ pair threshold, the LEP experiments also made precise measurements of the $W$-boson mass [17] $(\mechanical{M_W}=80.376\pm0.033\,\unit{GeV})$ and three-gauge-boson couplings [18].

## Physical quantities

| Symbol | Unit | Quantity | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| $\mechanical{\sigma},\mechanical{\sigma}_{\mathrm{peak}},\mechanical{\sigma}_{\mathrm{QED}},\mechanical{\sigma}_{\mathrm{L}},\mechanical{\sigma}_{\mathrm{R}}$ | $\unit{GeV}^{-2}$ | Cross sections | Annihilation cross sections in natural units |
| $\energy{s}$ | $\unit{GeV}^{2}$ | Squared energy | Square of the center-of-mass energy |
| $\mechanical{M_Z},\mechanical{M_W}$ | $\unit{GeV}$ | Boson masses | Neutral and charged weak-boson masses |
| $\energy{\Gamma},\energy{\Gamma_Z}$ | $\unit{GeV}$ | Decay width | Total width of the neutral weak boson |
| $G_{\mathrm{F}}$ | $\unit{GeV}^{-2}$ | Fermi constant | Low-energy weak-interaction constant |
| $\alpha$ | $1$ | Coupling | Fine-structure constant |
| $\electric{Q_q}$ | $1$ | Charge | Quark electric charge in units of the elementary charge |
| $L_i,R_i$ | $1$ | Chiral couplings | Left-handed and right-handed neutral-current couplings |
| $a_i,v_i$ | $1$ | Axial and vector couplings | Combinations of chiral neutral-current couplings |
| $\theta_{\mathrm{c.m.}},\theta$ | $1$ | Scattering angle | Angle between incoming and outgoing fermions |
| $z$ | $1$ | Angular variable | Cosine of the scattering angle |
| $\theta_{\mathrm{W}}$ | $1$ | Weak mixing angle | Angle that mixes neutral gauge fields |
| $x_{\mathrm{W}}$ | $1$ | Mixing parameter | Square of the sine of the weak mixing angle |
| $R,R_{\mathrm{peak}}$ | $1$ | Cross-section ratios | Annihilation cross section divided by the QED reference |
| $N_c$ | $1$ | Color count | Number of colors of the produced fermion |
| $N_\nu$ | $1$ | Species count | Number of light neutrino species inferred from the data |
| $A,A_{\mathrm{peak}}$ | $1$ | Asymmetries | Forward-backward asymmetry and its peak value |
| $P,P_{\mathrm{peak}}$ | $1$ | Polarizations | Net fermion polarization and its peak value |
